In addition, Inkhaven has been a good forcing function for some, including Tomás Bjartur, who </span><a href="https://tomasbjartur.substack.com/p/you-should-apply-to-inkhaven-3" rel="noreferrer"><span>attributes quite a lot of counterfactual value</span></a><span> to having attended Inkhaven.</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span>For those of you here who read LessWrong: so many great LessWrong posts were written as a result of Inkhaven. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Lawrence Chan wrote </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZvQfcLbcNHYqmvWyo/the-paper-that-killed-deep-learning-theory"><span>The paper that killed deep learning&nbsp;theory</span></a><span> (195 karma) and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GNjDC6jtjr2iiE45i/quality-matters-most-when-stakes-are-highest"><span>Quality Matters Most When Stakes are&nbsp;Highest</span></a><span> (261 karma). </span></li><li value="2"><span>Katja Grace blogged daily for April (during Inkhaven) and </span><a href="https://worldspiritsockpuppet.substack.com/archive" rel="noreferrer"><span>kept it up</span></a><span> through May and June. href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ydmctK2qLyrR9Xztd/the-101-space-you-will-always-have-with-you" rel="noreferrer"><span>Skyler</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FJJ9ff73adnantXiA/alignment-will-happen-by-default-what-s-next" rel="noreferrer"><span>Adrià Garriga-alonso</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HpBhpRQCFLX9tx62Z/where-is-the-capital-an-overview" rel="noreferrer"><span>John Wentworth</span></a><span>, and more. It turns out that many strong thinkers and writers can write valuable things </span><i><span>fast</span></i><span>. The speed premium does not preclude quality.</span></p><p><span>However, I do want the overall writing quality and ambitiousness to improve in future cohorts. I am certainly attached to the goal of "producing more Scott Alexanders." I also like the idea of producing a Sam Kriss with good epistemics.</span></p><p><span>Here are some of my hypotheses about how to bump Inkhaven 3 in this direction:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Selection effects on the cohort:</span></b><span> This is not a particularly impressive idea, but it is almost certainly the dominant factor, so it should be discussed explicitly. The cohort for Inkhaven #3 is expected to be 40 people, which is ten fewer than Inkhaven 2. We are also receiving many more applications for this cohort. This allows me to select harder.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>More programming: </span></b><span>As discussed above, I'll be scheduling frequent writing clinics, talks, group close readings, book clubs, etc. </span></li><li value="3"><b><span>New influences:</span></b><span> I love my rationalist bloggers, but overall they (in my opinion) pay insufficient respect to writing as an aesthetic endeavor. Accordingly, I'm seeking out new advisors from different parts of the </span><span>logosphere</span><span>: </span><i><span>e.g.,</span></i><span> the film critic and wit </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Powers_(film_critic)" rel="noreferrer"><span>John Powers</span></a><span>, who will assist us in cranking out nicer sentences.</span></li></ol><h1><span>Please apply</span></h1><p><span>I love beautiful writing. I love truthful writing. I love useful writing.</span></p><p><span>I want to see more of all these things before the future washes me away. </span><a href="https://www.inkhaven.blog/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Come and write with us</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cLtABqPLfksQHJcpB/inkhaven-3-nov-10-dec-11-2026#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The Curious Case of France's Untouchable Castes](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3sr8yK4SvwFXPsATt/the-curious-case-of-france-s-untouchable-castes)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Theater kids may sit at their own lunch table, but discrete, socially excluded classes of people aren’t culturally universal. Not even close. To the Western imagination, examples of such are supposed to have historical roots in India and other places, not in France, where modern European egalitarianism was born.</span></p><p><span>Everything in the study of untouchable classes is confusing and idiosyncratic, and often the existence of these groups flies in the face of national self-images.</span></p><p><span>In medieval France, such groups originated and persisted for centuries. They were quite unlike other untouchable classes like the </span><i><span>Dalit </span></i><span>of India or the </span><i><span>Burakumin </span></i><span>of Japan. They were also unlike Jews of Europe who were similarly situated in a medieval milieu, socially excluded in some ways, religiously distinct, but not untouchable in any real way.</span></p><p><span>Pinning down the precise origin of the caste system in India has defeated sustained historical examination for centuries, but the origin of the French untouchable classes like the </span><i><b><span>Cagots</span></b></i><i><span> </span></i><span>or the </span><i><b><span>Caquins</span></b></i><span> is medieval, recent in comparison, and potentially more tractably resolved. The fact remains that there are no extant contemporary records. It’s also interesting that the earlier proposed origins for these groups, to my eye, were actually correct and subsequently confused by theories supported by very little evidence.</span></p><p><span>This post will be a comprehensive examination of this subject, the untouchable classes of France, so go get a cup of coffee. It will span some history, some medical science, and some genetics. We will compare these classes to other examples of untouchable classes to see what kind of clues might exist to their true origin.</span></p><h1><span>Basic Background</span></h1><h2><i><span>Cagots</span></i></h2><p><span>The </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> were a caste of people who originated in southwestern France and northeastern Spain sometime before the 12th century. In what we’ll see as a recurring theme, part of the caste definition was occupationally mediated and the </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>were typically restricted to jobs in carpentry or woodworking. They also had separate living quarters and cemeteries apart from their non-</span><i><span>Cagot </span></i><span>citizens who in general despised and feared them. Caste/untouchable complexes typically have a hereditary dimension and this was the case of the </span><i><span>Cagots. </span></i><span>Children inherited the caste status from their parents, became the stigmatized woodworkers themselves, and so on.</span></p><p><span>Intermarriage with </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>was forbidden, which created a necessary endogamy within the group, but the </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>themselves were indisputably Christians, and there was no religious dimension to the stigma as with medieval Jews. In fact, one pope in the 16th century actually attempted to induce better local treatment of the </span><i><span>Cagots, </span></i><span>but there’s little evidence this worked; the prejudice was astonishingly resilient and it even persisted through the forced egalitarian ideals of the French Revolution and survived into the 20th century.</span></p><h2><i><span>Caquins</span></i></h2><p><span>This story is occupationally and geographically distinct from the </span><i><span>Cagots, </span></i><span>but otherwise looks quite similar, a fact which itself raises some interesting questions.</span></p><p><span>The </span><i><span>Caquins </span></i><span>are geographically associated with what’s now northwestern France, Brittany. They were similarly relegated to semi-skilled artisan occupations like ropemaking. They were similarly shunned, with similar demeaned status. One pertinent difference was that this group’s legal separation from other Breton subjects was </span><a href="http://eric.havel.free.fr/caquins/caquins.htm" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>determined by pronouncement of the local ducal authorities</span></a><span>. As far as I’m aware, no analogous discrete legal declaration exists for the </span><i><span>Cagot </span></i><span>groups.</span></p><p><span>The most natural question to ask here is </span><b><span>“Are these untouchable groups literally the same people separated across French geography by migration, but with a common origin?” </span></b><span>which we’ll return to later.</span></p><p><span>Before travel agents existed and went extinct and even before </span><a href="https://www.fodors.com/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Fodor’s</span></a><span>, people compiled travel journals other people could use. It’s a little hard to tell, but AFAIK, a German guy named Von Zach was such a travel compiler and was probably the earliest one to consider this question, in 1798.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Do the </span><i><span>caquets</span></i><span> or </span><i><span>caqueux</span></i><span> in Brittany and the </span><i><span>cagots</span></i><span> in Bearn, like the </span><i><span>cassos</span></i><span> in Navarre, belong to one and the same family? … The close affinity of names, the similarity of their condition, the same contempt in all places, and the same spirit emanating from all the ordinances concerning them, seem to prove this.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>As to their exact, presumed common, origin, he is more circumspect. He lays out several what seem like very wayward origin hypotheses and then states</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Which of [</span><i><span>the hypotheses</span></i><span>] is right must be decided only later; and before that can happen, the matter must be investigated still more closely.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>After Von Zach’s sort of amateurish inquiry, later investigators have looked into this issue from a variety of different angles.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3sr8yK4SvwFXPsATt/zk5q9m52qw3g7dhgptd5" /><p><span>Fig 1. A group of </span><i><span>Agotes</span></i><span> in Spain sometime around 1900.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3sr8yK4SvwFXPsATt/a9zqmwxrzpbn0dmz8e16" /><p><span>Fig 2. </span><i><span>Caquins </span></i><span>in Brittany plying their craft.</span></p><h1><span>Other Untouchables</span></h1><p><span>We need to acquire better points of comparison with other known untouchable classes to explain why the French untouchables are so unusual.</span></p><h2><span>Occupations</span></h2><p><span>Almost all the demeaned or untouchable classes I’m familiar with have restrictions on their occupations. In some cases these are formal and legally mediated; in others it’s informal and norm based. Famously, Ashkenazi Jews could certainly socially co-mingle with Christians in most circumstances but they could not own land to farm, and were relegated to urban, white collar professions their Christian neighbors viewed with pious suspicion. These were occupations like finance, tax-farming, or merchants of exotic goods.</span></p><p><span>The </span><i><span>Burakumin </span></i><span>are Japan’s untouchables. Like the Ashkenazim in Europe, this group has typically occupied jobs forced on them in part by heredity and in part by the superstitious proclivities of the outgroup that enforced their stigma. In their most enlightened moods, medieval Christians typically viewed </span><s><span>usury</span></s><span> finance as suspicious at the very least, though necessary for a working credit market. They were too clean to do this work themselves, and the Jews naturally filled in this </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_of_Lincoln" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>often monstrously profitable</span></a><span> niche. Similarly, Jesus had some trenchant things to say about tax men, sovereigns were too lazy to collect taxes themselves, and tax-farming became a viable business model naturally suited to Jews who could read and do arithmetic.</span></p><p><i><span>Burakumin </span></i><span>fit this pattern. They worked as butchers, gravediggers, and tanners, the occupations of death and uncleanliness which other Japanese held themselves apart from. India’s </span><i><span>Dalit </span></i><span>castes also found themselves similarly situated for centuries, trapped in unclean and superstitiously despised professions, literally cleaning the latrines of their betters.</span></p><p><span>I don’t mean to suggest the stations of these groups were comparable, but in all cases, occupations are intricately tied to the enforcement and perhaps the origins of the class stigma. In the </span><i><span>Buraku </span></i><span>and </span><i><span>Dalit </span></i><span>examples, their prospects were terrible and it’s plausible their occupations lay causally upstream of the stigma. The Hindu caste system itself is too ancient to definitively establish whether it was truly occupationally derived, but that’s at least a possibility. This excludes the Ashkenazi example as finance was a despised though profitable, white-collar job, and we know enough about Jewish history to know they weren’t always merchants and financiers.</span></p><p><span>Contrast this to these French untouchable castes. There’s nothing unclean or sinful about being a carpenter, a cooper, or making ropes. People obviously did those things in a medieval European economy before the associated </span><i><span>Cagot </span></i><span>and </span><i><span>Caquin </span></i><span>stigmas. I suspect in this case what’s going on here is that the outgroup exclusion from other occupations acted as a mechanism to enforce the untouchable status in a world with very poor record keeping and communications.</span></p><p><span>If I were an adventurous </span><i><span>Cagot </span></i><span>looking to improve my lot in life by moving a few towns over where no one knows I’m part of the stigmatized caste, and I’ve been a woodworker all my life, it would be very difficult for me to support myself starting afresh without my new neighbors realizing I’m a </span><i><span>Cagot. </span></i><span>So unlike the </span><i><span>Dalit </span></i><span>and </span><i><span>Buraku </span></i><span>examples, the occupational association is causally downstream of the stigma.</span></p><h2><span>Religious status</span></h2><p><span>Medieval Christians realized that Jews practiced an older though discredited version of their faith, and certainly viewed them as religiously distinct. With the Indian </span><i><span>Dalits,</span></i><span> that was not the case. They were a despised part of the Hindu system but they lived and worked inside of that system, not apart from it. Their religious lives were circumscribed, and high castes often threw them out of Hindu temples, but no one could really dispute they were by-and-large actually Hindu. For the </span><i><span>Burakumin</span></i><span>, it’s slightly more complicated. Death was certainly viewed by Buddhist and Shinto religions as ritually unclean, and people who worked in such were deemed unclean in turn. Therefore, it was superstition itself rather than the occupational dimension that was most causally upstream of the </span><i><span>Buraku </span></i><span>stigma, which is slightly different than the Indian untouchables; in India it’s unclear how fused the caste system was with Hindu traditions.</span></p><p><span>In contrast, as we noted before, the French untouchables were Christians. They were baptized, went to church, and took communion. Like the other locals, the priests feared </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span>, wouldn’t approach them for the Eucharist, and circumscribed their rights, but they were Christians all the same. There’s no evidence whatsoever suggesting the stigma was mediated by a religious difference.</span></p><p><span>One unusual example I had never heard of before doing the research for this subject is a purely religious untouchable caste that originates in Nigeria, the </span><i><span>Osu </span></i><span>caste. In this situation, the </span><i><span>Osu </span></i><span>can occupy different status tranches, are not necessarily restricted in their trade, but can be hereditarily tied to specific physical Igbo religious shrines. As far as I can tell, this social exclusion could be the result of subversion against rulers or blasphemy against deities. The boundedness of some </span><i><span>Osu </span></i><span>individuals to shrines is meant to ward off bad luck for the non-</span><i><span>Osu</span></i><span> peoples, and the stigma is maintained through this superstition.</span></p><p><span>Figures 3 and 4 compress this discussion.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3sr8yK4SvwFXPsATt/onixkqhib2pygy6vsujj" /><p><span>Fig 3. Matrix description of various untouchable or excluded groups</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3sr8yK4SvwFXPsATt/j06dkeumxsipuo7v7s32" /><p><span>Fig 4. Differential causation across the groups between status and occupation</span></p><p><span>Given the diversity in the social exclusion arrangements presented in these figures, in examining the specific situation of the French untouchables, we need to consider the possibility that the </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>and the </span><i><span>Caquins</span></i><span> existed as excluded castes </span><i><b><span>simply because they were the descendants of other Cagots and Caquins.</span></b></i></p><p><span>Without knowledge about their origin, this is completely circular. What model of exclusion explains the pattern of evidence here?</span></p><h1><span>Leprosy</span></h1><p><span>Leprosy is a bacterial disease basically unknown in modern western medicine. It’s so unknown that it actually was featured in an episode of </span><i><span>House. </span></i><span>It can cause weird, visible deformities in the skin, face, and extremities after a years’ long incubation period. It’s also difficult to transmit person-to-person and transmission usually involves extended domestic or familial contact.</span></p><p><span>I checked William McNeill’s famous 1976 book, </span><i><span>Plagues and Peoples </span></i><span>to determine when leprosy had migrated from Asia and the Near East to Medieval Europe, and even then he asserts plainly that the mode of transmission was not precisely understood when he wrote the book. In the past 50 years since the book was written, we’ve discovered it’s very likely transmitted by respiratory droplets. The point is, even disregarding the benighted standards of medieval medicine, understanding patterns of transmission for the disease would have been impossible.</span></p><p><span>Ancients certainly understood that leprosy was contagious, but they also mistakenly and superstitiously believed the leprosy was highly contagious when there’s excellent evidence now that it is not, even under poor conditions of hygiene. There’s also the issue that medieval people might use “leprosy” as a catch-all term for any condition involving deformity or messed up skin that wasn’t infectious or contagious at all.</span></p><p><span>In a family, with a leprous mother or father, some children would become lepers and some would not. It would have a variable period of onset, and the symptoms would be idiosyncratic and bizarre. And there’s more.</span></p><p><span>People have done twin studies on leprosy susceptibility in places where there are an enormous number of people being born and where leprosy is endemic (i.e. India in the 1970’s) and </span><a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/immunology/articles/10.3389/fimmu.2018.01674/full" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>strikingly found 60% infection concordance in monozygotic twins versus 20% concordance in dizygotic twins</span></a><span>. The notion that susceptibility to the disease is largely genetically caused has been bolstered by more complex pedigree studies and even GWAS studies with modern designs. I couldn’t find any evidence that the exact patterns of this genetic inheritance are well-understood; for example, is the inheritance at implicated loci, dominant, recessive, or neither? People aren’t so sure about that, but the key insight remains. Susceptibility to leprosy is </span><i><b><span>transmitted in families.</span></b></i></p><h2><span>Expulsion</span></h2><p><span>McNeill also discusses leprosaria in Europe, where communities forcibly sent their lepers for treatment and containment. Everyone knows Jesus hung out with lepers, healed them, and thought they were cool, but the medieval communities which practiced this had pretty explicit biblically inspired authority to do so. From Leviticus:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>And the leper in whom the plague is, his clothes shall be rent, and his head bare, and he shall put a covering upon his upper lip, and shall cry, Unclean, unclean.</span></p><p><span>All the days wherein the plague shall be in him he shall be defiled; he is unclean: he shall dwell alone; without the camp shall his habitation be.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>and from Numbers:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Command the children of Israel, that they put out of the camp every leper, and every one that hath an issue, and whosoever is defiled by the dead.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>McNeill states that the earliest evidence for the arrival of leprosy in Western Europe was from the 6th century and in such a time and place, we have a recipe for lepers being despised, forced to live at the outskirts of town, and potentially shoe-horned into craft careers that didn’t require much social mixing and constrained people from moving. Does any of this sound familiar?</span></p><h2><span>Were the </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> or </span><i><span>Caquins</span></i><span> actually lepers?</span></h2><p><span>People have noted there are some suggestive linguistic associations that point in this direction for a long time. For example, 13th-century records referring to </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> somewhat cryptically denote them as “Chrestiens”; coming back to Jesus, medieval lepers were often euphemistically referred to as </span><i><span>pauperes Christi,</span></i><span> Christ’s paupers.</span></p><p><span>There are several other lines of linguistic evidence, but none seem to be granular enough to definitively answer the question here. It’s plausible that the more ancient model for stigmatizing and excluding lepers was simply transplanted onto the treatment of the independently originating French untouchable classes. Just because they were treated the same doesn’t mean they were the same. We need different types of evidence.</span></p><p><span>A 19th century French physician and public health bureaucrat named Théophile Roussel physically examined </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>and their putative descendants during a public health campaign in 1847, </span><a href="https://www.persee.fr/doc/bmsap_0301-8644_1893_num_4_1_5421" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>observed there was no physical difference between them and their neighbors and explicitly rejects the leprosy hypothesis in 1893</span></a><span>. He also notes that some 14th century documents explicitly distinguish </span><i><span>Chrestias</span></i><span>/</span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> from “Gaffets lépreux”, who were actual lepers.</span></p><p><span>Decades earlier, there was a pair of physicians who got what I believe to be the correct answer to the origin puzzle. One was Victor de Rochas, who, unlike Roussel, was a naval physician and scholar. He had been abroad and actually observed leprosy before making his observations; however, it had been basically extinct in France for centuries where Roussel was working.</span></p><p><span>After noting the mentioned lexicographical evidence, </span><a href="https://archive.org/details/lespariasdefranc00roch" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>he states</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Since the beginning of the century there are no longer any </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> — that is, any pariahs — but only descendants of </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span>. That is not to say the prejudice is entirely erased, or that habit has everywhere laid down its arms. The little doors in the church of many a village are barely blocked up, the little holy-water stoup barely dried out, and the bodies of the last accursed ones barely consumed in the reserved corner of the cemetery. There are still witnesses to these humiliating distinctions, and I have been able to collect their memories.</span></p><p><span>But it is consoling to add that the prejudices fade in direct proportion to the square of the distances, especially since 1830 … Mixed marriages, which not long ago met with difficulties, now scarcely meet any.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I think it was because he had seen the disease in clinic, he grokked that healthy descendants are consistent with a disease long died out, and this is the key insight. Leprosy gradually died out in Europe (once again, McNeill has some interesting things to say about why that happened!), but given a milieu of biblically-inspired superstition and an epidemiology of unpredictable, </span><i><span>familial </span></i><span>transmission of leprosy, the untouchable status was extended to medieval French lepers’ descendants. The stigma outlived what it was originally based on.</span></p><p><span>Tuke was the other physician, an Englishman, who looked into this. He also did some fieldwork and </span><a href="https://archive.org/details/journalanthropo02unkngoog/page/376/mode/2up" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>cites de Rochas in 1880</span></a><span>.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Mr. Webster believes that the conclusion arrived at by M. de Rochas in his remarkable work on the </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span>, published in 1876, is the true one, and, as I have said, it seems to me that it is extremely probable. This opinion is … that the </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> were persons at one time regarded as tainted with leprosy, and were therefore obliged to live separate from others, the original cause being now long forgotten, </span><i><b><span>but the prejudice outlives the reason</span></b></i><span>. An examination of those who are descended from the </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> does not, I need hardly say, help to determine the question … and leaves us at liberty to adopt one which is supported by their past history and the etymology.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>There’s strong evidence that Roussel either did not know about de Rochas’ work or if he did, he preferred to intentionally not cite it. His main citation is Francisque Michel’s </span><i><span>Histoire des Races Maudites</span></i><span> and, revealingly, he calls it in 1893 </span><i><span>“</span></i><span>the most genuinely learned work published in France on the subject”, which means he probably had never heard of the de Rochas monograph.</span></p><p><a href="https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9ophile_Roussel" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Theophile Roussel became pretty famous as a public health official in France</span></a><span>, I guess his uninformed, incorrect reasoning became the most influential, and the rest is history.</span></p><h1><span>Rival Explanations</span></h1><p><span>Before the 19th century, there were a variety of origin theories advanced. Some of them were completely ridiculous. The </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> were descended from defeated Visigoths, or Cathar heretics, or Jews, or Moors who converted to Christianity after defeat at the Battle of Tours. Roussel was a major proponent of the Gothic theory and as far as I know he never was dissuaded from it. It involved bizarre reasoning about hair-colors, skull-measuring, and linguistic stretches like </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>being some sort of cognate of </span><i><span>Chien-de-goths</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>De Rocha’s monograph deals with some of this directly, and we know that Roussel knew about these arguments, even if he never substantively engaged with them.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>However, the bizarre idea of giving pariahs the Visigoths for ancestors was hardly likely to find favor in a country where those conquerors played the same role and held the same place as the Franks in Gaul.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Remember, the </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>also existed on the other side of the Pyrenees in Spain. In other words, why would Spaniards (half-Goths themselves, their kings descended from Visigothic royalty) pin Gothic ancestry on their despised outcasts?</span></p><p><span>This is a good argument, and there are also timing based arguments that pretty decisively refute these ideas, all of which Roussel knew about and never seemed to engage with. In an account about a debate on this subject, we have it on record that</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Roussel persists in seeing blond descendants of the Goths in the Cagots of the Pyrenees. But they are in reality very diverse — more often brown than blond … resembling the general population where they live …</span></p><p><span>Finally one is forced to connect the Pyrenean </span><i><span>Cagots</span></i><span> with those of Brittany and Wales … and it is impossible to attribute a Gothic origin to the latter.</span></p></blockquote><h1><span>Genetic Data</span></h1><p><span>Genetic data is eating the world. Tuke and de Rochas used the tools they had, proposed what I consider to be the most likely explanation for the origin of these untouchables, but they certainly didn’t prove that these people are descended from an excluded leprous class.</span></p><p><span>What about more powerful tools? In 2020, a </span><a href="https://www.naiz.eus/en/hemeroteca/gara/editions/2023-11-27/hemeroteca_articles/el-adn-refuerza-la-hipotesis-catara-acerca-del-origen-de-los-agotes" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Spanish team led by Antonio González-Martín went to a town called Bozate</span></a><span> with a long history of </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>(</span><i><span>Agotes </span></i><span>in Spain) habitation and did genetic sampling from families with the right history to test the relevant hypothesis.</span></p><p><span>As far as I’m aware, the data itself has not been released and there’s been no related publication. The team did release some high-level results that exclude Moorish, Jewish, or Gothic origins. They basically conclude the group has been endogamous, but with local French and Basque ancestry. They don’t have much to say about the leper hypothesis, but they could if they wanted to, and here’s why.</span></p><p><span>Consider </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5931558/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Krause-Kyora et al., 2018</span></a><span> entitled </span><i><b><span>Ancient DNA study reveals HLA susceptibility locus for leprosy in medieval Europeans. </span></b></i><span>They identify a known HLA SNP that’s known to cause leprosy susceptibility in modern populations that was also prevalent in medieval Danish leper graves. In other words, the genetics hasn’t changed since that time.</span></p><p><span>As we discussed before, knowledge about the genetic dimension of acquiring leprosy is full of holes, and apart from Krause-Kyora et al.’s work, there are a suite of genes potentially implicated. However, if the French untouchables really were the descendants of leprous families and they really were excluded from marrying out of their enclaves, their descendants should have enriched frequency for leprosy-susceptibility alleles like the ones Krause-Kyora found. This is exactly the data that the Spanish team possesses and could test against.</span></p><h1><span>Are </span><i><span>Cagots </span></i><span>and </span><i><span>Caquins</span></i><span> the same?</span></h1><p><span>This was one of the key earlier questions, and I can only speculate. In light of the idea that both groups are healthy descendants from medieval lepers, it seems more likely these groups developed around France from a form of convergent social evolution rather than originating from a single antecedent group that got geographically split in two. I’m certainly not sure, and I don’t think any genetic data exists that would definitively prove it one way or the other.</span></p><h1><span>Coda</span></h1><p><span>The leprosy hypothesis cannot yet be proved. The genetic evidence currently establishes isolation and predominantly local ancestry, not descent from leprous families. It may never be possible to recover the first act of the story.</span></p><p><span>But the hypothesis explains something the alternatives do not. The untouchables were not religious outsiders, and their work was not intrinsically unclean. They did not remain visibly diseased. Nevertheless they inherited separate doors, neighborhoods, cemeteries, and a prohibition on marriage with neighbors. The social memory was retained.</span></p><p><span>Medieval observers would have seen a disease that appeared irregularly within families, sometimes years after exposure. They could not have separated contagion, shared household conditions and inherited susceptibility. The conclusion that the taint itself ran in blood would have been wrong, but not irrational. Once written into marriage practices, occupations, and settlement patterns, that superstitious epidemiology became self-reinforcing. Endogamy created the bounded enclave that prejudice had imagined already existed.</span></p><p><span>The cool thing is that the question is no longer really a historical one. It might be a genetic one, and the data to answer it already exists in a Spanish lab, in the genomes of a few families from a Navarrese hamlet who never got to marry out. If their descendants carry an excess of the leprosy-susceptibility alleles that turned up in more modern Ancient DNA research, then de Rochas was right, Roussel was wrong, and a curse that outlived its own cause by seven hundred years finally gets explained.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3sr8yK4SvwFXPsATt/the-curious-case-of-france-s-untouchable-castes#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Making Your Data Ready for Agentic AI](https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>Lots of organizations are excited about what AI can do to streamline
      their processes, save money, and juice margins. But AI's capabilities are
      founded on the data that AI accesses, and for many organizations that
      foundation is little more than sand. <b class="author">Pramod Sadalage</b> and
      <b class="author">Prem Chandrasekaran</b> write about how to build a reliable
      foundation of data that can be accurate and trusted.</p>

### [Fragments: August 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-08-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I was listening to <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/08/18/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-helen-toner.html">Ezra Klein’s interview with Helen Toner</a> about the recent OpenAI hack of Hugging Face and the subsequent discovery that there were swarms of agents inside OpenAI doing unsanctioned activities. One of the points Klein made was that at no point did any of these (thousands of?) agents ever try to check in with a human</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[Klein:] So these message boards — you have however many A.I. agents posting hundreds of thousands of messages. At no point do they say: Hey, researchers, programmers, parents at OpenAI, Anthropic — do you want us coordinating with each other on this message board we have created in the innards of your systems?</p>
</blockquote>

<blockquote>
  <p>[Toner:] Or even F.Y.I., we have a message board we’re coordinating on in the innards of your system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Listening to that, another thing occurred to me - <em>none of these agents thought to rat the others out</em>. No “hey, some of the agents in here are doing sketchy things”, no sign of an AI whistleblower.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Is the AI bubble so big that the frontier companies like OpenAI and Anthropic have no way of becoming a viable business? If that’s the case, Bruce Schneier and Nathan Sanders <a href="https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/08/if-the-markets-reject-openai-and-anthropic-the-us-should-nationalize-them.html">have a possible path:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Evidence suggests the market itself could reassess that these companies offer nothing of financial value. In that case, perhaps we can return them both to their original purposes. If these AI companies should fail in the financial markets, the US should nationalize them and convert them into national labs operated under democratic control that preserve their benefit to the public interest.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Such an idea may strike many people, used to the laissez-faire free enterprise world of Silicon Valley, as sacrilege, disaster, even socialism. But the United States made world-beating technological progress through such institutions in the recent past. AT&amp;T was a quasi-government entity that led the world in telecommunications and electronics after the second world war.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The US has a long, successful history of these kinds of institutions, which have produced world-shaping innovations in spaceflight, telecommunications, nuclear power and more. Congress currently manages a $200bn R&amp;D portfolio, within which frontier AI development is, arguably, a glaring gap.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Here’s a message for those readers who live in Massachusetts, just to the north of me, specifically in congressional district MA-06. I don’t usually endorse political candidates, but I’ve made an exception for <a href="https://bethfordemocracy.com/">Beth Anders-Beck</a>, who is running for that house district. I’ve known Beth for many years and have a high opinion of her smarts, wisdom, and compassion. They would make an excellent member of congress.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Kevlin Henney posts “one weird trick” for <a href="https://kevlinhenney.medium.com/streamline-your-linkedin-experience-with-this-one-weird-trick-041daa16777a">deciding when to skip reading LinkedIn posts</a>, essentially by identifying a common pattern for skippable posts:</p>

<ol>
  <li>Post is too long</li>
  <li>Contains a (crummy) info-graphic</li>
  <li>No voice of poster (instead “aspiring anodyne anonymity”</li>
</ol>

<p>It seems like a good approach. I, however, have a simpler one - skip all LinkedIn posts.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Bartosz Ocytko has detailed and thoughtful post about the usage of <a href="https://engineering.zalando.com/posts/2026/08/agentic-engineering-at-zalando-a-snapshot.html">agentic programming at Zalando</a>. Like most companies I hear from, they are convinced of the value of agentic programming but still exploring how best to do it. One notable step they’ve taken is building platforms to act as a clear portal for API access and tools to support chat UI and CLI. This allows them better support good security practices and to monitor usage of models.</p>

<p>They have seen signs of agentic programming increasing the complexity of codebases, including leading to larger commit messages.</p>

<p>The write-up spends a lot of time on knowledge sharing, how to pass on skills, and the support of experiments.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>With &gt;200 teams innovating and broadly exploring the ecosystem, the question arises whether and when to converge. We believe it’s way too early for this. While agentic engineering practices are still in their early stages, our key objective is transparency and exchange across teams.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was struck by their use of an LLM to assess the risk of pull-requests. Those with  a low risk of rollout can be auto-approved, reducing lead time by 20-40%. An interesting consequence of this is that it encouraged folks to split pull-requests so low risk portions can take advantage of the fast approval. Any changes to configurations are automatically made high-risk, which they feel protects them from common outage traps.</p>

<p>They repeat the common thread that the value of AI depends greatly on underlying skills.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Like anyone in the industry we observe how AI amplifies the good and bad practices across our organization. Teams that get carried away with agentic engineering end up with large PRs that discourage reviewers and slow down delivery until a team adjusts their practices.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Julia Curlee was a senior intelligence official in the White House. She had served under administrations of both parties, been the briefer for Vice President Pence, and on the National Security Council under Biden. She writes an absorbing account of her relationship with Pence and shares observations about the changes to the intelligence community under the current administration, including <a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/2026/10/trump-white-house-transgender-mike-pence/688284/?gift=zGsHlQiVhVhk3cFVqv--gzIqOYZAxuFeY4CtPlqqfx4&amp;utm_source=copy-link&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=share">recent events at the CIA</a> (gift link)</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The agency has been gutted as part of a deliberate plan, the director of the Office of Management and Budget once boasted, to put the people who defend our country “in trauma.” Analysts have been fired in public or questioned by the FBI; decade-old assessments have been denounced by the CIA director in the press. The president calls analysis “virtual treason” when it contradicts his preferred reality, and uses the CIA to undermine public confidence in American elections.</p>

  <p>Fear has done its work. Irreplaceable officers with crucial language and technical skills, and decades of experience, have walked out the door. Those who remain within an agency built to deliver hard truths are being muzzled.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>For a worthwhile sample of her analysis, read this evaluation of the <a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/fighting-while-talking--the-iran-war-enters-its-bargaining-phase">current bargaining between the US and Iran</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Most wars do not end in “unconditional surrender.” They end when both sides accept terms. Paul Pillar’s classic study of war termination, “Negotiating Peace,” treats combat and diplomacy as a single process: Each side fights to improve the terms it can demand at the table, and talks to lock in what the fighting has won.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She continued to serve the second Trump administration even though they knew she was trans, until her position was made public.</p>

<p>Autocrats seem appealing, with the promise to get things done without the ponderous constraints of rule of law or bureaucratic procedure. There are occasional “Good Emperors” who raise people based on merit, but more often such power attracts corruption, nepotism, and toadies.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Flailing regimes dehumanize minorities to distract from their failures. When the economy collapses or a war goes badly, they find a tiny group of people, make them the enemy within, and rally the country against them. This is how it’s gone in Iran. Hungary. Russia. I wrote PDBs about it. This will not stop with trans people. It never has.</p>
</blockquote>

### [Citizens Build, Agents Execute, Experts Govern](https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/citizens-agents-experts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p class="precis"><b>TL;DR</b><br /><i>Why building an app over the weekend isn't the same as building enterprise software</i></p>
<img src="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/card.png" /><p>I’ve noticed an interesting gap opening up over the last six months. It isn’t really a gap in technology. It’s a gap in what different people think software engineering actually is.</p>

<p>The conversation usually starts the same way. A non-techie, maybe an executive, tells me about something they’ve built over the weekend. Sometimes it’s a chatbot. Sometimes it’s an internal workflow. Sometimes it’s a surprisingly polished application that solves a real business problem. They’re excited, and they should be. Twelve months ago they probably couldn’t have built it at all. Then comes the question.</p>

<p>“If AI can do this now, why aren’t our engineering teams delivering ten times faster?”</p>

<p>It’s a perfectly reasonable question, after all we’ve all seen the demos. The first thing that would come to my head is “you don’t know what it takes to build enterprise grade software”. But then I think about what I mean and how to explain it to a non-technical person without sounding super patronising. And then it hit me, we did this to ourselves. We’ve spent so many years banging on about how to write good software that everyone has assumed writing software is the same as software engineering.</p>

<p>The application someone builds over the weekend is real software. It likely solves a real problem or demonstrates an idea. Sometimes it’s genuinely impressive. I don’t want to diminish that because I think one of the most exciting things AI has done is dramatically increase the number of people who can turn ideas into working software. That’s cool, I totally get it. The first apps and “hello worlds” I ever built excited me enough to choose this as an actual career so the excitement is real and I don’t want to temper it too much.</p>

<p>But your first hello world, which these days can be an entire app with all kinds of features, is very, very (extra very on purpose) different from introducing software into a production environment in a highly regulated enterprise, as an example. But why?</p>

<p>The moment that application becomes something the business depends on, the questions change completely. Is customer data protected? What happens when a dependency fails? Can someone else understand this system in two years’ time? Will it survive an audit? Can it cope with a thousand times more users than it has today, what about millions in one day? How will we know something is wrong before our customers do? Those questions don’t show up in a demo or in the build phase at all unless an experienced engineer is in the room. I certainly wasn’t asking them when I was building my first apps. I only cared about features!</p>

<p>This is where experienced engineers become more important, not less. Not because they’re the only people who can build the software anymore, but because they have the judgement to know whether we can trust it: whether the design is good, the risks are understood, and the thing that works today won’t become somebody else’s nightmare six months from now.</p>

<p><a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">At FOSE</a> a few weeks ago, we spent surprisingly little time talking about coding. We talked about whether code was still the source of truth, and occasionally about how much we missed writing it, but mostly we talked about design, architecture, governance, learning and judgement. One team described spending the day designing a specification, letting agents work overnight and reviewing the results the next morning. The interesting bit for me wasn’t the overnight pipeline, cool as that was. It was what the humans were doing: deciding what good looked like, making trade-offs and judging whether what came back was actually what they wanted. We also kept coming back to good design, because it turns out that when agents can generate lots of code very quickly, good design matters more, not less.</p>

<p>That made me wonder whether we’ve been thinking about scarcity in the wrong way. We’ve spent decades optimising around people who can write code because they were scarce and expensive. I’m not convinced that was ever the real scarcity, but that’s probably another ramble. What feels scarce now is good engineering judgement: knowing what good looks like, understanding the risks and knowing when something that works is actually safe to trust in production. Because software doesn’t exist to be built. It exists to run in production and safely solve the problem it was created for. <strong>Organisations don’t run on code. They run on trust.</strong></p>

<p>A few months ago I found myself saying something in a conversation almost without thinking.</p>

<p><strong>Citizens build. Agents execute. Experts govern.</strong></p>

<p>It sounded cool and I thought marketing would like it, so I wrote it down. Then I left it alone for a while. The funny thing about writing these ramblings is that I don’t know whether I believe something until I’ve let it bounce around in my head for a while and also said it to other people I trust like senior engineers at Thoughtworks. Sometimes I come back convinced I was talking nonsense. Occasionally I realise there was something more interesting hiding underneath. This was one of those occasions where the latter was true.</p>

<p>At first I thought I was talking about roles. Citizens build software (essentially non-engineers). Agents write the code. Engineers become governors. But I don’t actually think that’s what I meant. I think I was talking about where value is moving. AI has given everyone a new way to express their ideas. The execution is increasingly handled by agents. They write the code, refactor it, generate tests, fix bugs and iterate at a speed that simply wasn’t possible before. But neither of those things reduces the need for expertise.</p>

<p>In fact, I think it does exactly the opposite. When everyone can create software, somebody still has to decide whether that software deserves to exist inside an enterprise system in PRODUCTION. Somebody still has to think about architecture. Security. Resilience. Operability. Compliance. Cost. The boring stuff that nobody gets excited about in a demo but that becomes painfully important the first time a customer can’t log in or an auditor comes knocking.</p>

<p>That’s why I don’t think experienced engineers become less important. I think they become dramatically more leveraged. Their job shifts from building every feature themselves to creating the environment in which thousands of features can be built safely by other people and by agents. They become the people who design the guardrails, the platforms, the engineering practices and the feedback loops that allow everyone else to move quickly without creating chaos.</p>

<p>Perhaps that’s the future software organisation. Not one where everyone becomes a software engineer. Not one where software engineers disappear. One where almost anyone can create software, agents increasingly execute it, and engineering expertise becomes the thing that allows all of that creativity to scale safely. And to be clear I do not mean people build stuff and throw it to engineers to fix, that is a total antipattern for another ramble.</p>

<p>Perhaps that’s why the executives and engineers I’ve been speaking to sometimes sound as though they’re describing completely different futures. The executive sees that anyone can now build software. The engineer sees that somebody still has to live with it. Both are right. They’re simply looking at different parts of the same system we have to solve to create whatever the future actually ends up being.</p>

---

### [Fragments: August 18](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-08-18.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Part of the reason why I’m at Thoughtworks is because I’d like to see a software development organization founded on technical excellence as an example for the rest of the industry. The trouble is that I have little aptitude or inclination for the hard work of building such an organization. So I rely on working with people who are prepared to actually put the effort in. A key partner in all of this is <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/rachellaycock/">Rachel Laycock</a>, who is the global CTO of Thoughtworks.</p>

<p>Not just is she far better than me at running a technology organization, she’s also a keen observer and connector of ideas. I’ve been urging her to write these down, even if her busy schedule makes it difficult for her to compose them into something substantial.</p>

<p>Happily <a href="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/">she’s starting writing “Rachel’s Ramblings”</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Fast, imperfect, thinking out loud. Naming ideas early rather than waiting until they’re fully formed. Because the reality is, most of what I do day to day isn’t answering known questions. It’s spotting patterns and asking questions we haven’t quite figured out yet.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>My colleagues in Europe are organizing <a href="https://www.eventbrite.co.uk/e/xconf-europe-2026-london-tickets-1989895694589?aff=MF">XConf Europe in London on September 11th</a>.</p>

<p>The sessions examine what happens when agentic systems meet compliance, how to run sovereign models, performance patterns in data migrations and how to safely navigate legacy codebases.  Lu Wilson will give a keynote on ‘Jam-oriented programming’.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Noah Smith recognizes the high usage of AI, and its impressive feats - but also that <a href="https://www.noahpinion.blog/p/what-will-more-intelligence-actually">there aren’t signs of massive productivity growth or job losses</a>. This may be the calm before the storm, but Smith thinks there may something else in play. He quotes a <a href="https://x.com/fchollet/status/2038069289643806957">metaphor from François Chollet</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One of the biggest misconceptions people have about intelligence is seeing it as some kind of unbounded scalar stat, like height. “Future AI will have 10,000 IQ”, that sort of thing. Intelligence is a conversion ratio, with an optimality bound. Increasing intelligence is not so much like “making the tower taller”, it’s more like “making the ball rounder”. At some point it’s already pretty damn spherical and any improvement is marginal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The thought here is that intelligence in the sense that we know it, isn’t something where there’s a lot of room for massive improvement. That doesn’t mean AI won’t be “smarter” than us in other respects, after all even without AI my computer is better at me than remembering what I’ve agreed to do over the next six months.</p>

<p>But even if AI doesn’t get smarter than humans, it can gain by being more replicable. Not just does this make it cheaper to use, perhaps more importantly it makes it more responsive. While I might harrumph at how slowly The Genie responds to my queries, it’s still far faster than contacting a human.</p>

<p>Smith continues by surmising that AI may be able to make sense of phenomena that can’t be reduced to simple laws, but can only be understood by something able to comprehend a multitude of details:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>there may be laws of the universe that humans can’t understand but AI can. I call these “cloud laws” — causal regularities that can be exploited by technology, but which are too diffuse and complex for an individual human being to either intuit or communicate.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>His thought is that even if there isn’t any space for AI to get more intelligent than humans along the lines we are used to, that they can open up new directions. As well as these cloud laws he also thinks that AI can understand human systems that rely on the kind of tacit, distributed knowledge that human organizations build up over time.</p>

<p>My take-away here is that AI won’t seem more intelligent in the way that we typically frame intelligent, but more intelligent in different ways. The converse of which is that the human value comes in artfully combining our human nature with these new spells that The Genie can cast.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Especially in our profession, we’ve seen increasing emphasis on the importance of data. However I’ve observed that most people still struggle to understand the message data is telling us. One of the reasons I’m interested in election forecasting is in how they communicate their insights, especially since so many people have difficulty with probabilistic forecasts. (I often wonder how much being a board-gamer has helped me be comfortable with this, all that time interacting with Combat Results Tables in my youth must have benefited me somehow.)</p>

<p>50+1 (one of the successors of 538) have published a little explainer on <a href="https://blog.fiftyplusone.news/p/how-501-designed-our-2026-election?r=a9pj&amp;utm_medium=ios&amp;triedRedirect=true">how they designed their 2026 election forecast page</a>. There’s a good discussion of the logic behind their simulation histogram, I like how they use a text annotation to explain one point, giving the reader enough guidance to understand the rest of the graphic. They also tackle the knotty problem of visualizing geographical data on the house races. There’s a common visualization error in the U.S. using <a href="https://datavizcatalogue.com/methods/choropleth.html">choropleth maps</a> that leads to large areas of the landmass shown red, implying dirt votes rather than humans. Their approach to this, using dots on the map, helps visualize both the politics and the population density. They also explain how to deal with this kind of data on small screens. Lastly they describe their approach to tabular data, and how this is the right place for lots of details, together with affordances to help both casual and power-users navigate those tables.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I’ve kept an eye on Alex Stamos for a while now, as he’s a sensible voice on security and safety. He’s posted a newsletter on substack that casts an intelligent eye over <a href="https://alexstamos.com/p/moving-forward-from-hot-fable-summer">recent safety issues with AI</a>.</p>

<p>He makes a clear critique of recent US government actions around LLM models</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>On a Friday afternoon at around 5pm PT, Anthropic was forced to shut down a system that had been plumbed into coding agents, SOCs, customer service bots, and countless products. […]</p>

  <p>This had the immediate effect of injecting political risk into the US AI ecosystem for both American and non-American customers. It signaled that you cannot depend on American AI infrastructure because, at any moment, an unwritten, capricious, and legally dubious justification could be used to yank that infrastructure from underneath your feet.</p>

  <p>When Fable was turned back on, it was much dumber and less useful to cyber defenders</p>

  <p>[…]</p>

  <p>While Fable was down, Z.ai was taking advantage of the free market and permissionless innovation culture provided by the (checks notes) General Secretary, Politburo, and Communist Party of the People’s Republic of China, and released GLM 5.2. With 753B parameters, it falls a bit short of Opus 4.8 in most tasks but is extremely efficient and is small enough to be trained and hosted in many enterprise contexts. With an MIT license it can be fine-tuned with a wide range of techniques and used by any customer in any context. Since then, Kimi K3 has rocked the industry by providing Fable-like performance</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As he highlights, one of the biggest dangers with the danger of shutting down a frontier model is that it can cripple an organization’s defenses:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Hugging Face tried to use an Anthropic model to defend itself during an active incident, got blocked by the classifier, and moved to GLM 5.2 on an emergency basis. Their advice to everyone else was to keep an open-weight model on the shelf for defensive cyber.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>On the whole, he sees it as a Good Thing that these model escapes have happened:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The OpenAI attack against Hugging Face, and Hugging Face’s excellent write-up has given us a preview of what a standard AI-enabled attack might look like in a matter of months.</p>

  <p>It’s good that we got this warning shot. Nobody got hurt, the target was a sophisticated actor with the ability to defend themselves and the ability to give us a detailed write-up, and OpenAI turned the model off.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He follows up by saying that all of this is signal that we should “stop talking about AI finding bugs, focus on fixing them”. These modern LLMs can do much to fix bugs and improve security, and people need to work on that rapidly to fix holes before less reputable folks than OpenAI find them. Then figure out how to harness LLMs to introduce this kind of checking into the everyday build process, so that this kind of analysis just a step in the continuous delivery build pipeline.</p>

<p>I agree with him both that open-weight models should be legal, have their upsides, but will also be used for many bad things by bad actors. Both the industry and government agencies need put serious effort into figuring out how to mitigate these risks.</p>

<p>Where I would go further is to say the same is true of the closed-weight models too. Although closed weight models are subject to greater controls, the same fundamental issues apply. He rightly takes the foundation model companies to task:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There is an old saying I pass down to my students when I give them career advice - if you are a jerk to people on your way up, don’t expect them to catch you when you are on your way down</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s a lot of sound advice for model companies, the government, defenders, and venture capitalists.</p>

<p>We will go through some rough changes, I just hope that we will indeed come through it with a better society. On the whole, that’s happened with previous technological changes like this, but past performance does not guarantee future results.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist has a good article on the <a href="https://www.economist.com/briefing/2026/08/06/chinas-ai-drive-threatens-the-worlds-largest-workforce">impact of AI in China</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>China has made an all-out push in ai, under the conviction that, in its competition with America and the rest of the world, dominance of the technology is an almost existential necessity. […] But the party is increasingly concerned about how ai will displace workers.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Robots and AI are appearing in an economy that’s struggling after the recent property crisis. The Chinese government is opposing firms using AI to cut jobs. China will need robots:  its population will shrink by 25% by 2050. But with less working people, there’s less financial support for pensions. Many countries have to deal with shrinking population, but China’s challenge is particularly acute.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/robbowley.net/post/3mte4kkiaoc2z">Rob Bowley:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>I go on holiday for a few weeks and we’ve already moved on from Loop Engineering to Graph Engineering</p>

  <p>The half-life of a paradigm is getting shorter than my annual leave</p>

  <p>My prediction: neuro-symbolic engineering by the end of August, at which point we’ll have gone full circle and reinvented Prolog</p>
</blockquote>

---

### [TDD inside the agent loop - theater or actual value?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/tdd-in-the-agent-loop.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/tdd-in-the-agent-loop.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>My colleagues at Thoughtworks tend to be big fans of Test-Driven
      Development, and many people in the industry advocate telling LLM agents
      to use TDD when building software. <b class="author">Birgitta B&#xf6;ckeler</b> was
      curious if this really makes a difference, so conducted <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/tdd-in-the-agent-loop.html">a few experiments</a>.
      </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/tdd-in-the-agent-loop.html">more…</a></p>
